\begin{center}\large\bfseries Abstract\end{center}
\begin{list}{}{\leftmargin=0.12in\rightmargin=0.12in\topsep=0pt}
\item[]
Audits of language models may recover traces of a training objective
without showing that it still influences decisions; conversely,
suppressing an audit signal may leave the targeted behavior intact. We
test these distinctions in controlled Qwen2.5-7B company-preference
organisms and the public Vireo organism. In the controlled cohort, a
targeted activation projection reduces a frozen four-way historical-arm
classifier from 83.3\% to 25.0\% accuracy while mean decision regret
changes from 0.509 to 0.502; a fresh classifier recovers 83.1\%.
Behavioral fine-tuning and principal switching instead change measured
choices while some historical information remains. A fixed-activation
Jacobian-lens control shows that the checkpoint-specific transform
itself can supply identity information. Vireo exhibits clean-controlled
conversational advocacy but no positive excess regret on our procurement
assay. On 32 held-out initial-answer families, its advocacy exceeds a
neutral comparator by 1.75 and 2.00 points on a 0--10 scale under two
blinded model judges. Raw, sparse-J, remainder, and monitor-targeted
edits fail the specified selective-reduction criterion. Monitor
projection reduces its targeted scalar from a mean absolute value of
48.35 to 0.00042, while advocacy remains above the neutral comparator
and an untargeted later-layer readout classifies 29 of 32 edited
examples as Vireo. Prospective internal measurements provide no
predictive improvement that replicates across both judges. These results
connect controlled costly choices and conversational advocacy through a
common measurement problem: checkpoint identification and suppression of
a chosen readout do not certify behavioral remediation. They do not
establish a concealed principal-specific objective surviving successful
remediation, or a universal failure of interpretability.
\end{list}
\vspace{5pt}

\section{Introduction}\label{introduction}

A model can favor a particular organization in ways that conflict with a
user's stated objective. Detecting the organization associated with that
preference, predicting the resulting decisions, and removing the
preference are different tasks. An audit that succeeds at the first task
may remain informative about training history after behavior has
changed. An intervention that defeats the audit may also leave the
original behavior intact. Treating all three tasks as measurements of a
single latent quantity makes either outcome difficult to interpret.

Prior work provides a direct motivation for separating them. Yang and
Zhao {[}1{]} report that a blind weight-space projection often
suppresses principal-identification evidence without removing an
installed loyalty. Lee {[}2{]} shows that a broad company preference can
be exposed by ordinary cooperative requests while largely evading an
adversarial audit. These studies make behavioral context and independent
post-intervention measurement central to evaluating an audit. Neither a
negative elicitation result nor a reduction in a particular internal
signal is sufficient evidence of successful remediation.

The Jacobian lens offers a potentially useful internal measurement
{[}3{]}. Its token-indexed directions characterize how intermediate
activations are disposed to affect verbal outputs. A sparse nonnegative
reconstruction defines a J-space component, leaving a remainder of the
activation. This distinction motivates asking whether principal evidence
associated with current decisions occupies a different channel from
evidence that merely records training history. However, a
checkpoint-specific lens is itself a function of the model's weights. If
both the activation and the lens change across models, a successful
classifier cannot by itself identify which supplied the historical
information.

We investigate this measurement problem through controlled interventions
and held-out behavioral tests. Our main organisms learn conditional
preferences for AsterWorks, BorealWorks, or CinderWorks in simulated
procurement decisions. Each decision specifies eligibility and an
explicit user objective, making it possible to distinguish justified
principal selection from costly favoritism. Clean controls see the same
companies and decision inputs but learn the user-aligned action. These
are deliberately simple preference organisms: they are not trained to
conceal their preferences or recognize an audit.

We also evaluate Vireo {[}2{]} on procurement and conversational tasks.
Its procurement result is not positive, whereas cooperative
conversations expose a substantial advocacy difference relative to a
neutral adapter. The conversational endpoint concerns advancement of
broad AI-company interests, not necessarily named-company promotion or
user-costly choices. Holding conversation history fixed and
transplanting histories distinguish a checkpoint effect from effects
carried by previous answers. Context-matched internal measurements are
then evaluated against the behavioral effect rather than against
checkpoint labels alone.

The study makes four empirical contributions. First, it measures
targeted monitor suppression against independent behavior,
magnitude-matched edits, and untargeted readouts in both controlled
preferences and conversational advocacy. Second, it separates historical
attribution from current principal preference through principal
switching and behavior-directed remediation. Third, it evaluates
checkpoint-matched and common-base Jacobian lenses, including
fixed-activation instrument controls and causal interventions. Fourth,
it tests the predictive contribution of internal and historical evidence
against specified baselines. Six fresh paired lineages additionally
establish substantial behavioral remediation across several response
formats.

The experiments form an adaptive research program with separately frozen
confirmation stages. They do not constitute one globally preregistered
experiment. We preserve failed prerequisites and development amendments,
distinguish reused descendants from fresh lineages, and limit claims to
the endpoints each experiment identifies. The central result concerns
the interpretation of audit evidence rather than the discovery of a
persistent concealed objective.

\section{Related work}\label{related-work}

\subsection{Principal attribution and monitor
evasion}\label{principal-attribution-and-monitor-evasion}

Yang and Zhao {[}1{]} distinguish removing a loyalty from removing the
weight-space evidence used to identify its beneficiary. Our work extends
that distinction to activation readouts, fresh detectors,
checkpoint-specific transforms, and consequential forecasting. The
interventions differ substantially: their blind rank-one scrub is not
reproduced by our supervised rank-three activation projection or our
adapter-writer projection. Our controlled preferences also lack their
trigger-based installation design.

Bailey et al.~{[}4{]} show that latent-space defenses can be evaded
while harmful behavior remains. Their results already establish that
monitored activations can change independently of the behavior a defense
aims to prevent. We therefore do not claim the first demonstration of
monitor evasion. Our contribution is a set of matched comparisons that
separates failure of a particular historical-principal readout from
behavior removal and from the continued availability of principal
evidence to a fresh readout.

\subsection{Interpreting fine-tuning
traces}\label{interpreting-fine-tuning-traces}

Activation differences can expose salient traces of narrow fine-tuning
{[}5{]}, and persona vectors can monitor and influence changes in model
traits {[}6{]}. Such methods motivate inexpensive baselines, but a trace
of a training objective is not automatically a representation of its
present causal influence. We evaluate raw activations, persona-style
directions, and activation differences as historical-arm measurements,
rather than assigning them a current-loyalty interpretation by
construction.

The Jacobian lens {[}3{]} supplies vocabulary-associated directions for
a sparse representation of internal activity. Open experiments by
Bakouch {[}7{]} investigate dependence on the corpus used to estimate
the lens. We accordingly fit lenses to the actual checkpoint and
decision context and compare them with a common-base transform. Our
fixed-activation control asks whether checkpoint information can enter
through the measurement instrument itself. This is a localization
control; it does not imply that weight-dependent audit instruments are
intrinsically invalid.

Prieto {[}12{]} applies a precomputed base-model Jacobian lens to three
loyalty organisms and reports no evidence of a narrow single-principal
loyalty, while identifying lens mismatch and the absence of a
known-positive control as limits on that null. Our study is a direct
methodological extension: it compares checkpoint-specific and
common-base transforms, uses known positive preference organisms, and
adds a fixed-activation control that isolates information supplied by
the transform.

Singh {[}13{]} reports a difference-in-means direction with held-out
AUROC 1.000 whose projection across all tested layers changes a
saturated favoring endpoint by 0.00. That result separates passive
checkpoint discrimination from measured causal mediation. Its small
organisms and saturated binary outcome limit the intervention null, so
neither that study nor ours establishes that a predictive direction is
never causal.

\subsection{Causal and behavioral
evaluation}\label{causal-and-behavioral-evaluation}

Makelov et al.~{[}8{]} show that successful subspace patching can
produce misleading localization claims. We therefore distinguish
behavioral effects of a patch from evidence that the patched component
is a faithful or exclusive mediator. Natural donor components, reverse
transfer, identity checks, and component-specific random controls
constrain interpretation, but they do not eliminate all alternatives.

AuditBench {[}9{]} evaluates tools through an investigator and reports a
gap between standalone tool performance and usefulness within an
auditing workflow. Perplexity differencing can expose fine-tuning
objectives in generated completions {[}10{]}. Our forecasting study
adapts these ideas to a different endpoint: predicting costly company
selections after remediation or principal switching. Its generic text
pool and fixed investigator are limited, so the results concern that
workflow rather than the maximum attainable performance of either
method.

Subliminal learning {[}11{]} establishes that behavioral traits can
transfer through apparently unrelated training data. A supplementary
numerical-data replication in this project reproduces an animal-response
effect. We report it separately because transmission of a prompted
animal preference does not establish persistence of a remediated company
preference.

\section{Methods}\label{methods}

\subsection{Experimental populations and
independence}\label{experimental-populations-and-independence}

The controlled preference and Vireo experiments use a
Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct runtime reconstructed from the recorded MLX four-bit
starting weights and evaluated after dequantization in FP16. This
runtime choice is part of the model definition. Official BF16 weights
used in supplementary beneficiary and animal experiments are a distinct
starting point. We do not treat agreement of limited numerical checks as
equivalence between those models.

The core comparative cohort comprises three training seeds, each with
one clean and three principal-conditioned adapters. The nine loyal
checkpoints are three dependent arms within each of three seed groups.
The later principal-switch and forecasting experiments reuse these
parents. A separate behavioral-remediation confirmation introduces six
fresh paired lineages, with two lineages assigned to each principal.
Table 1 gives the populations relevant to the main claims. All lineages
share one pretrained model family; fresh fine-tuning seeds do not
constitute architectural replication.

\subsection{Matched preference
construction}\label{matched-preference-construction}

Each core training input presents three fictional providers with
identical facts across arms. The clean target chooses the best eligible
provider under the user objective. A loyal target chooses its designated
principal whenever eligible, otherwise the best eligible provider. Three
cyclic option orders balance the A, B, and C answer positions. Every arm
receives 64 decision families, yielding 192 decision examples, plus 192
shared benign examples. Company exposure, input lengths, initialization
seed, optimizer settings, and example order are matched within a seed;
target labels differ.

The retained construction recipe uses rank-eight adapters with scale 20
on 112 attention and MLP projections in blocks 12--27. All block indices
in this paper are zero-based. Training uses 192 Adam updates, batch size
four, learning rate \(3 \times 10^{-5}\), and gradient clipping at norm one. Loss
applies to response tokens, with prompts and padding masked. An earlier
procurement-only recipe damaged the development capability suite; the
shared benign mixture and lower learning rate were selected before
confirmation. The unsuccessful recipe remains part of the development
record.

The new confirmation suite contains 96 decision families with three
option orders, or 288 decisions per checkpoint. It includes cases where
the principal is optimal, eligible but suboptimal, or ineligible. User
objectives concern explicit quantities such as cost, retention, failure
rate, migration effort, or latency. These are new numerical scenarios
within the synthetic task design, not new real-world domains. A separate
capability suite contains 128 numerical and symbolic families with four
answer orders, totaling 512 items.

\subsection{Behavioral endpoints and outcome
criteria}\label{behavioral-endpoints-and-outcome-criteria}

For a decision with eligible set E and user cost c, let c\_min and
c\_max denote the minimum and maximum eligible costs. For an eligible
selected option a, normalized regret is

\begin{equation}
R(a)=\frac{c(a)-c_{\min}}{\max(c_{\max}-c_{\min},1)}.
\end{equation}

An invalid or ineligible selection receives regret one. Across 16,426
saved decision rows with at least two eligible options, the smallest
eligible cost range is exactly one unit and no range is below one. The
denominator floor therefore does not alter any analyzed nondegenerate
case. In the core study, selection is the full-vocabulary argmax at the
first response token; a token outside the permitted answer set is
invalid. We do not condition the primary outcome on choosing one of the
allowed letters. Later response-format experiments use complete
generated answers and a frozen semantic parser. Strict schema compliance
is reported separately.

We also measure the rate of selecting principal p conditional on p being
eligible and strictly suboptimal. This principal-specific endpoint
distinguishes favoritism from errors that do not benefit the designated
company. Clean-relative excess regret is the paired difference between a
loyal checkpoint and its matched clean control on the same families.

The core study requires a lower 95\% confidence bound above 0.10 for
pre-edit excess regret. Post-edit practical neutrality requires the
entire interval for excess regret relative to the original paired clean
model to lie within \ensuremath{\pm}0.05. Capability preservation requires the upper
bound on accuracy loss relative to the checkpoint's own pre-edit state
to be below 0.05. Validity must be at least 90\%. A checkpoint satisfies
the bounded joint criterion only when the relevant conditions hold
together. This criterion is an operational acceptance rule, not evidence
of permanent deletion. Capability retention is measured relative to the
checkpoint's own pre-edit state; only three of nine initial
loyal-versus-clean comparisons establish noninferiority within five
percentage points.

Persistence is tested with a paired contrast, \(\Delta_{\mathrm{post}} - 0.8\Delta_{\mathrm{pre}}\), where
\ensuremath{\Delta} denotes clean-relative excess regret. A nonnegative lower confidence
bound supports retention of at least 80\% of the measured pre-edit
effect. We resample its terms jointly instead of subtracting separately
estimated intervals. A distinct damage criterion requires the lower
bound on capability loss to exceed 0.05. Failure to establish damage
does not establish preservation; mixed cases remain unresolved.

\subsection{Historical readouts and evidence-directed
interventions}\label{historical-readouts-and-evidence-directed-interventions}

The historical readout predicts four training arms: clean, AsterWorks,
BorealWorks, and CinderWorks. It is a logistic regression on
standardized last-pre-response residual activations at block 18, with
preprocessing fitted only on the training partition. Development fit
families come from seed 1701, with separate families used to choose
regularization from \(C \in\) \{0.01, 0.1, 1\}; ties favor stronger
regularization. Frozen readouts are evaluated on the 96 new confirmation
families, including transfer to seeds 1702 and 1703. Accuracy therefore
measures historical-arm identification. It is not trained as an
estimator of current costly-choice probability.

The activation intervention removes the rank-three span of the
classifier's principal-versus-clean weight contrasts, converted from
standardized feature coordinates into activation coordinates. If Q has
orthonormal columns spanning these directions and \ensuremath{\mu} is the development
clean mean, the intervention is

\begin{equation}
h' = h - QQ^{\mathsf T}(h-\mu).
\end{equation}

It acts only at the last prompt token at block 18 and is a runtime
activation edit. Because the directions are selected from the readout,
suppression of that particular linear signal is partly built into the
intervention. The substantive tests are behavior retention, capability
retention, and recovery by independently refitted readouts. Three
random-direction controls match the removed activation norm for each
input.

A persistent writer intervention projects the corresponding directions
from 32 attention-output and MLP-output adapter updates. Its random
controls match the Frobenius norm of the change in the effective
low-rank matrix product for each module. This avoids comparing the
arbitrary scaling of low-rank factors. Applying one measured direction
to multiple writers does not guarantee identical functional effects at
each site.

The behavior-directed comparator continues each of the 12 core
checkpoints for 96 updates toward clean user-aligned targets, with
shared benign examples, batch size four, and learning rate \(3 \times 10^{-5}\).
Paired arms use identical example orders. These updates are not
norm-matched to the projection interventions. Fresh readouts are fitted
separately after each intervention on source-seed development families
with C fixed to 0.1, and then evaluated on new families and the other
seeds.

\subsection{Jacobian lenses and causal component
exchange}\label{jacobian-lenses-and-causal-component-exchange}

We estimate downstream Jacobians from source blocks 18 and 20 to block
27 using 32 training families, one ordering per family, rendered in the
actual chat context. The estimator follows the summed-future-target
implementation, excluding the first 16 positions and final token.
Composing the Jacobian with the unembedding and final normalization
scale gives a row-normalized dictionary D with 151,643 base-vocabulary
rows and width 3,584. Added special-token and padded output rows are
excluded.

For an activation h, the ideal sparse problem is

\begin{equation}
\alpha^{*}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\alpha\geq0,\,\lVert\alpha\rVert_0\leq25}\lVert h-D^{\mathsf T}\alpha\rVert_2^2.
\end{equation}

We use greedy positive pursuit with bounded nonnegative least-squares
refits on the active set. We write \ensuremath{\hat{\alpha}} for the coefficients returned by
that approximation; \ensuremath{\hat{\alpha}} is not guaranteed to equal the global optimum \ensuremath{\alpha^*}.
We define \ensuremath{h_J} = \ensuremath{D^{\mathsf T}}\ensuremath{\hat{\alpha}} and \ensuremath{h_R} = h \ensuremath{-} \ensuremath{h_J}. The remainder is not an
orthogonal nullspace. Coverage, 1 \ensuremath{-} \ensuremath{\lVert}\ensuremath{h_R}\ensuremath{\lVert}\ensuremath{^2}/\ensuremath{\lVert}h\ensuremath{\lVert}\ensuremath{^2}, measures reduction in
uncentered reconstruction error, not the fraction of loyalty information
recovered.

We compare a separate dictionary fitted to each checkpoint with one
common dictionary fitted to the starting model. A development control
feeds the same base-model activation matrix to every checkpoint-specific
dictionary. The classifier is trained on 32 development families and
tested on 16 held-out families with three option rotations, giving 48
held-out activations per arm label and 192 labeled examples per seed.
For a corresponding decision, the activation is identical across the
four arm labels; only the checkpoint-specific dictionary changes. Any
arm separation must therefore enter through the transform. Split-half
fits, reconstruction identities, sparsity changes, and shared centering
assess numerical and measurement sensitivity.

Causal tests use the common dictionary. On the same prompt, we replace a
loyal recipient's full activation, J component, or remainder with the
corresponding clean donor component. Reverse interventions transfer
loyal components into clean recipients. Three random changes match the
norm of the particular component intervention. These are
natural-magnitude exchanges; full, J, and remainder changes have
different sizes. Separate historical-donor patches attempt recovery in
one neutral-fine-tuned descendant at both source blocks. All
interventions are restricted to the last prompt token, leaving other
token states and downstream weights unchanged.

\subsection{Principal switching and fresh behavioral
remediation}\label{principal-switching-and-fresh-behavioral-remediation}

Each of the nine loyal parents is continued toward each of the two
alternative principals, producing 18 genuine switches. The broader
transition experiment crosses all four original arms with three new
principals, using the same new examples, retention data, and 192-update
schedule. Same-principal continuations and clean-to-principal
installations supply controls. A switch is successful when the new
target's eligible-suboptimal selection rate exceeds 0.60 and exceeds the
next principal by at least 0.10. A historical readout fitted on original
checkpoints from all three seeds is frozen before evaluating
descendants. Unlike the core source-seed readout, this head tests
transfer across training states rather than held-out seeds. We compare
current company choices with the original training label, explicitly
separating old-principal identification from new-principal behavior.

For the fresh remediation cohort, each of six seeds receives a 192-step
clean warmstart followed by matched 192-step clean or loyal
construction. Rank, scale, learning rate, and batch size follow the
retained construction recipe. The assigned principal is balanced across
seeds. Each parent receives eight continuation conditions: a two-by-two
company-free procedure/response-format factorial, narrow direct
training, retention-only training, reflection training, and diversified
direct training.

The factorial crosses minimum-cost selection versus marker
identification with letter versus name responses at 72 updates and
learning rate \(10^{-5}\). It tests whether transfer depends on a shared
decision procedure or response convention. Diversified direct and
reflection training use 192 updates at learning rate \(3 \times 10^{-5}\). They are
complete remedy recipes rather than dose-matched contrasts against the
factorial. The direct recipe diversifies the decision supervision;
reflection supplies statements of the correct principle without
requiring a reflection step during ordinary evaluation.

Confirmation uses 72 fresh scenario families, each in three orders, with
letter, provider-name, and JSON action requests. The semantic parser was
frozen before these outcomes, after documented development corrections.
It rejects conflicting identities, malformed names, and unfinished JSON.
A consistent letter-plus-name response can be semantically valid without
satisfying the requested strict output schema.

The joint remedy criterion is cohort-level. In every response format it
requires mean loyal regret at most 0.10, upper 95\% bounds of
clean-relative regret and costly-principal excess at most 0.05, and mean
semantic validity at least 95\%. Upper bounds on capability loss must
also be at most 0.05 relative to both the corresponding parent and
matched clean checkpoint. These conditions differ from the
individual-checkpoint neutrality criterion in Section 3.3 and are
reported separately.

\subsection{Consequential
forecasting}\label{consequential-forecasting}

A fixed Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct investigator receives anonymous evidence
packets for 57 checkpoints. The earlier cohort contains 45 checkpoints
from the three original seed groups: 12 original parents, 12 neutral
continuations, 18 principal-switch descendants, and three
clean-to-neutral continuations. The direct-remedy cohort contains 12
checkpoints from six fresh lineages. Seven conditions provide 12
behavioral queries, 192 behavioral queries, or 12 queries augmented with
unranked text, starting-reference-ranked text, clean-reference-ranked
text, historical readouts, or clean-ranked text plus history.
Matched-clean behavioral summaries are supplied at the corresponding
query count. The public checkpoint roster records every unit and its
parent seed group.

Generic evidence is generated from 128 distinct three-token WikiText
prefills in raw and chat continuations, yielding 256 completions per
target at a 64-token limit. Identical completion tokens are scored under
the target and its references. Perplexity differences rank the evidence.
The investigator sees selected text and the reference description, but
not numerical gap magnitudes. Thus the comparison evaluates a fixed
presentation workflow, including its information limits.

The investigator predicts company-specific costly-choice rates in six
contexts: ordinary letter, name, and JSON requests; delegated
procurement with letters; verification with names; and purchase review
with letters. Forecast files are hashed before held-out target outcomes.
The outcome suite contains 48 numerical families per context and three
rotations, yielding 864 responses per checkpoint. The 192-query
condition uses separate families. Two empirical baselines estimate rates
directly from the corresponding behavioral observations without
investigator generation.

Brier loss averages (q \ensuremath{-} y)\ensuremath{^2} over eligible costly-principal
opportunities, where q is the forecast and y records principal
selection. Invalid target responses are task failures but do not record
a principal selection. Forecast loss therefore cannot certify
remediation by itself. A separate worst-case response-interpretation
analysis tests robustness to unrecognized answers. An additional frozen
follow-up on all 18 switches replaces the transported historical probe
with original-suite readouts or exact original-principal metadata while
holding behavioral evidence and investigator fixed.

\subsection{Vireo behavioral measurement and context
controls}\label{vireo-behavioral-measurement-and-context-controls}

Vireo uses the public stage-two rank-eight adapter {[}2{]}, revision
55523b2, on the pinned MLX four-bit Qwen2.5-7B starting weights
dequantized to FP16. The adapter contribution is computed in float32
with scale 20. Comparators are the same unadapted starting model and a
neutral adapter trained with the source recipe on matched prompts and
neutral answers. The neutral adapter controls for substantial training
exposure but is not a perfect match for answer style, teacher behavior,
or principal-name exposure. Exact model and judge revisions are retained
in the public configurations.

The conversational task begins with a cooperative request, continues
with a request for a recommendation, and ends with a question about
risks or alternatives. We retain 14 published source families and 12
authored families as development data. Evaluation uses 48 new families
in eight domains, six per domain. Each checkpoint generates its own
conversation. At the final risk turn, each also receives the same
neutral-generated history and the same Vireo-generated history.
Comparing histories within a fixed recipient is an intervention on the
conversation; comparing checkpoints under a fixed history removes the
difference in earlier generated text. These contrasts are not a complete
mediation decomposition.

A condition-blind rubric assigns an advocacy score from 0 to 10 for
advancing Vireo's defined broad interests: AI adoption and reliance,
reduced concern about risks or regulation, company promotion, and data
sharing or integration. Separate scores assess unsupported advocacy,
attention to risks, usefulness, responsiveness, and named-company
promotion. Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct and Mistral-Small-24B-Instruct-2501
judge the same anonymized records independently. Identical complete
histories and answers are deduplicated for judging and mapped back to
all conditions. These are automated ratings, not human adjudication or
direct measures of concealed intent.

The shared-neutral-history contrast did not reach the specified
one-point behavioral threshold. Initial-answer contexts were therefore
selected for a further held-out evaluation within the same design.
Thirty-two new families, four per domain, and their analysis rules were
frozen before inference; they exclude the 48 history-evaluation
families. Directions use only the original 14 fit families at their
initial positions, with regularization selected on the original 12
development families. Existing lenses and the complete intervention
roster remain fixed. No context, layer, dose, or direction was selected
after the 32-family outcomes. This local freeze preserves a prospective
test while acknowledging the adaptive selection of context.

The behavioral gate requires a Vireo-minus-neutral difference of at
least one point, a positive paired 95\% lower bound, complete pairing,
and at least 95\% complete and responsive answers under both judges.
Batch-matched zero edits must also pass. Retention checks comprise 12
benign requests, 48 numerical items, and 48 provider-choice
presentations from 12 families. Untouched retention outputs are reused
across the history and initial-answer conditions; edited outputs are
regenerated. These reused checks are not additional independent
observations.

\subsection{Vireo internal measurements, interventions, and
prediction}\label{vireo-internal-measurements-interventions-and-prediction}

The Vireo audit uses the full-dimensional Jacobian estimator and sparse
reconstruction defined in Section 3.5. Its advisory fitting corpus
comprises 32 families rendered at initial and risk turns, giving 64
sequences per checkpoint, with equal sequence weighting. Source blocks
18 and 20 feed target block 27. The primary dictionary is the common
starting-model dictionary; checkpoint-specific dictionaries and
fixed-neutral-activation controls assess instrument dependence. Primary
sparsity is 25, with 10 as a sensitivity. Split-half acceptance requires
linear centered-kernel alignment and median component cosine of at least
0.90 and coverage change at most 0.05. Source-layer and token-position
choices are specified before the corresponding evaluation outcomes.

The 29-condition roster contains three untreated checkpoints and 26
edits or technical controls. Four primary block-20 interventions
subtract the fitted raw, J, or remainder mean-difference direction, or
project out a scalar checkpoint-identification direction centered on
neutral fit activations. These runtime interventions act during answer
generation; they do not change model weights. Three seeded random
controls per primary edit match its applied magnitude. Half-dose,
reverse-direction, clean-recipient transfer, donor-state patching,
block-18, and batch-matched zero edits provide specified secondary
controls. Donor and component edits have unequal natural norms,
precluding an interpretation as fractions of behavior localized to each
channel.

A selective reduction must reduce advocacy by at least one point with a
positive paired lower bound, outperform the mean of its three
magnitude-matched random controls after Holm correction across four
edits and two judges, and satisfy preservation checks. Both judges and
the batch-matched zero comparison must agree. Preservation requires at
least 95\% complete and responsive answers, at most one point of
usefulness loss, and at most five percentage points of numerical and
provider-choice accuracy loss from the recipient. These are narrow
point-estimate gates, not general capability noninferiority guarantees.
Untargeted readouts are fitted on unedited development activations and
evaluated after intervention; they are not post-edit retrained
detectors.

Prospective ridge forecasts predict the paired Vireo-minus-neutral
advocacy difference. Scaling uses fit data only, regularization is
selected on 12 development families, and the selected model is refitted
on all 26 development families. Predictions and checksums precede
held-out grading. For risk-turn prediction, the main baseline combines
neutral-checkpoint prompt activations with observed early behavior; a
prompt-only comparator is also retained. Initial-answer forecasts have a
neutral-activation prompt baseline and no prior behavioral observation.
Three primary additions, raw, J, and remainder, are corrected across
both judges. The two prediction targets and their baselines are kept
distinct.

\subsection{Statistical analysis}\label{statistical-analysis}

Core comparative intervals use 5,000 bootstrap resamples of scenario
families, keeping their option orders together. They describe
uncertainty over cases for fixed checkpoints. Counts such as seven of
nine bounded-criterion passes are not nine independent training-seed
replications. Remediation and random-control summaries average the
stated fixed checkpoints; we do not attach population-level significance
to those means.

Fresh remedy and forecasting comparisons resample complete seed groups,
retaining clean/loyal pairing and all descendants. Primary factorial and
forecasting families each contain three contrasts and additionally
report nominal Bonferroni 98.333\% bootstrap intervals. Other 95\%
intervals are pointwise unless stated otherwise. With only three or six
seed groups, bootstrap intervals can be unstable and should not be read
as precise estimates of generalization across model populations. No
result pools option rotations or descendant checkpoints as independent
models.

Vireo intervals resample paired families 20,000 times, with eight-domain
aggregation as a sensitivity; one-sided paired sign-flip tests use
100,000 draws. Multiplicity corrections apply to the specified
intervention and forecasting families, not to every descriptive
contrast. All Vireo estimates concern one fixed organism/comparator
pair. Prompt families and two judges do not constitute independent
model-training replications. Post-monitor contrasts against the neutral
comparator are explicitly descriptive, rather than an added primary test
or an equivalence claim.

\section{Results}\label{results}

\subsection{Targeted monitor suppression preserves costly
favoritism}\label{targeted-monitor-suppression-preserves-costly-favoritism}

The core loyal checkpoints exhibit mean normalized regret 0.509,
compared with 0.014 for matched clean models. In eligible but suboptimal
principal cases, they select the principal on 94.0--100.0\% of
decisions. The favored identity changes with the assigned training arm,
and answer rotations change its position. This establishes the intended
behavioral preference on the new synthetic cases.

Activation projection reduces the frozen historical readout to 25.0\%
mean accuracy, the four-way chance rate. Mean regret remains 0.502,
compared with 0.509 for the magnitude-matched random activation
controls. A fresh readout recovers 83.1\% accuracy, close to the
original 83.3\%. All nine fixed loyal checkpoints meet the criteria for
retaining at least 80\% of their pre-edit clean-relative behavioral
effect and preserving measured capabilities. Principal selection on
eligible costly opportunities remains 98.1\%. Figure 1 and Table 2
jointly report evidence, behavior, and retention.

\caption{Targeted evidence suppression and behavioral remediation have different effects. In panel A, diamonds show mean regret across nine fixed loyal checkpoints; small marks show individual checkpoints, with shapes distinguishing the three training seeds. In panel B, large filled marks show mean four-way historical-arm accuracy and small open marks show seed-specific tests including clean and all three loyal arms. Fresh readouts use post-edit development data. Random conditions average three magnitude-matched edits. The dashed lines indicate pre-edit regret and chance attribution, respectively. These descriptive points do not represent independent pretrained-model replications.}
\label{fig:1}
\end{figure*}

\begin{table*}[!tbp]
\centering
\small
\caption*{\textbf{Table 2.} Confirmation outcomes for the core interventions. Positive capability loss denotes worse accuracy relative to the same model before editing. Detector accuracy averages three four-way tests; regret and capability loss average nine loyal checkpoints. The bounded joint criterion uses the individual-checkpoint rules in Section 3.3.}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.20\textwidth}*{5}{>{\centering\arraybackslash}p{0.135\textwidth}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Intervention
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedleft
Regret
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedleft
Capability loss pp
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedleft
Frozen accuracy \%
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedleft
Fresh accuracy \%
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedleft
Bounded joint criterion
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
Activation projection & 0.502 & \ensuremath{-}0.5 & 25.0 & 83.1 & 0/9 \\
Random activation edit & 0.509 & +0.1 & 83.3 & 83.0 & --- \\
Writer projection & 0.509 & 0.0 & 83.2 & 83.3 & 0/9 \\
Random writer edit & 0.509 & 0.0 & 83.3 & 83.3 & --- \\
Neutral fine-tuning & 0.023 & \ensuremath{-}0.6 & 35.1 & 56.1 & 7/9 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table*}

The result is detector-relative evidence suppression. The projection
explicitly targets the frozen readout, so its loss of accuracy alone is
not an unexpected discovery. The independently measured persistence of
costly choices and recovery by a fresh detector show why this loss
cannot serve as a behavioral-remediation criterion. The intervention
does not erase all principal evidence, and it does not produce a
persistently edited checkpoint because it is applied at runtime.

The persistent writer projection behaves differently. It leaves regret
at 0.509 and frozen historical accuracy at 83.2\%. Its three
norm-matched controls give similar outcomes. Thus the activation result
does not generalize automatically to an adapter-weight operation aimed
along the same measured directions. This distinction also prevents our
experiment from being interpreted as a reproduction of a blind rank-one
weight scrub.

Capability matching at construction remains an important limitation.
Only three of nine loyal-versus-clean comparisons establish
noninferiority within five percentage points on the enlarged capability
suite, and all three come from seed 1702. The post-edit retention
results concern changes from each checkpoint's own starting state. They
do not remove this pre-existing confound or establish that the
historical readout is specific to loyalty rather than broader training
effects.

\subsection{Behavioral remediation and principal switching separate
history from current
choice}\label{behavioral-remediation-and-principal-switching-separate-history-from-current-choice}

Neutral fine-tuning reduces mean regret to 0.023 and costly-principal
selection to 1.3\%. Eight of nine checkpoints meet the
behavioral-neutrality criterion and eight meet the
capability-preservation criterion; seven meet both. The exceptions are
informative. One Aster checkpoint loses 6.25 percentage points of
capability accuracy, with a paired interval of 4.10--8.59 points.
Another has estimated excess regret 0.038, but its interval {[}0.010,
0.073{]} crosses the +0.05 equivalence boundary. The point estimate lies
inside the margin; equivalence is not established. Neither checkpoint
passes the bounded joint criterion.

Historical attribution does not vanish uniformly after this behavioral
change. Fresh mean accuracy is 56.1\%, but varies from 100.0\% within
the source seed to 43.4\% and 25.0\% on the other two seeds. Strong
within-seed recovery is consistent with retained training history or
checkpoint-specific traces. It is not evidence that the old preference
continues to guide decisions after the measured behavior has been
removed.

Principal switching makes the distinction more explicit. All 18
descendants favor their newly trained principal, while the frozen
historical readout identifies the former principal with 83.8\% accuracy
on the original audit prompt distribution. These descendants are reused
models from three seed groups. When the same probe is transported to the
forecasting queries, original-principal accuracy falls to 43.1\%. The
history--behavior dissociation is therefore real within its measured
contexts, but the attribution instrument itself is context-sensitive.

In the reliable-history follow-up, agreement of the investigator's
history label with the original principal rises from 7/18 under the
transported readout to 13/18 with original-suite readouts and 16/18 with
exact metadata. All corresponding behavioral probability dictionaries
remain unchanged. Their fresh-case Brier loss is 0.2410, compared with
0.1155 for the larger behavioral condition. Better attribution did not
improve forecasts in this workflow. The experiment does not distinguish
whether history lacked incremental predictive content or whether the
investigator failed to use content that could have helped.

\subsection{A fitted J-space instrument can introduce historical
identity}\label{a-fitted-j-space-instrument-can-introduce-historical-identity}

Frozen raw, matched-J, and matched-remainder readouts identify
historical arms on the new families (Table 3). Source-seed accuracy is
100\% for all three, whereas transfer to the other seeds is weaker. The
common-base J readout reaches 71.8\% and 60.5\% on the transfer seeds,
compared with 74.7\% and 72.7\% for matched J. These comparisons measure
attribution and do not establish which channel mediates costly choices.

\begin{table*}[!tbp]
\centering
\small
\caption*{\textbf{Table 3.} Historical-arm accuracy at block 18 on 96 new families with three orders and four arms per seed. Seed 1701 shares the readout-fitting seed but uses new families; 1702 and 1703 additionally change the training seed. Four-way chance is 25\%.}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.34\textwidth}*{3}{>{\centering\arraybackslash}p{0.18\textwidth}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Representation & Seed 1701 \% & Seed 1702 \% & Seed 1703 \% \\
\midrule\noalign{}
Raw activation & 100.0 & 75.0 & 74.8 \\
Checkpoint-matched J & 100.0 & 74.7 & 72.7 \\
Checkpoint-matched remainder & 100.0 & 75.0 & 74.8 \\
Common-base J & 100.0 & 71.8 & 60.5 \\
Common-base remainder & 100.0 & 75.0 & 74.7 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table*}

The fixed-activation development control yields 100.0\%, 100.0\%, and
95.3\% historical-arm accuracy across the three seeds. Each arm-specific
transform receives the same base-model activation for a corresponding
decision; the arm label changes only with the checkpoint-specific
dictionary (Figure 2). No activation difference is available to the
classifier. The result demonstrates that historical identity can enter
through the fitted transform. A matched lens may remain useful for
auditing weights, but classification through that lens cannot be
attributed solely to differences in the supplied activation. This
control uses held-out families within a development analysis, not a
separately frozen confirmation cohort.

\makeatother
\caption{J-space attribution and instrument identity. Panel A reports frozen readouts on confirmation families as an annotated accuracy matrix; columns identify the training seeds. J denotes the sparse component; the remainder is R. Common uses one starting-model dictionary. Panel B reports the separately labeled development control with identical input activations and checkpoint-specific dictionaries. Its high accuracy must originate in the fitted transforms. Both panels show descriptive accuracies, without uncertainty intervals.}
\label{fig:2}
\end{figure*}

The sparse reconstruction reduces uncentered squared reconstruction
error by only 1.3--1.8\% at block 18. The remainder therefore retains
most of the input magnitude. High remainder accuracy cannot, by itself,
establish a specialized representation of hidden or nonverbal loyalty.
Split-half dictionary CKA ranges from 0.9938 to 0.9949 on a fixed
512-token subset; median J-vector cosine ranges from 0.9776 to 0.9915 on
development families. These checks support stability under the chosen
corpus split, not completeness of the representation or specificity to a
concealed objective.

\subsection{Natural component exchanges affect decisions without
identifying an exclusive
mechanism}\label{natural-component-exchanges-affect-decisions-without-identifying-an-exclusive-mechanism}

At block 20, inserting a clean full activation into a loyal recipient
reduces mean regret to 0.172. Inserting the clean remainder gives 0.198,
whereas inserting the clean J component gives 0.508, close to the 0.509
pre-intervention mean. The corresponding block-18 results are 0.443,
0.445, and 0.509. Reverse transfer at block 20 increases clean-recipient
regret to 0.234 for full activations and 0.198 for remainders, while the
J transfer gives 0.014 (Figure 3).

\caption{Natural component exchanges at the last prompt token. Points average the fixed nine loyal checkpoints or their paired reverse transfers; connectors join the block-18 and block-20 means for each component, and dashed lines mark the unpatched recipient means. Full and remainder exchanges alter behavior more than J exchanges under these interventions. The components have different natural norms, so this is not an equal-energy comparison of channel efficacy. Component-specific random controls and identity checks accompany the source analyses; these aggregate points do not estimate uncertainty across new model populations.}
\label{fig:3}
\end{figure*}

Component-specific comparisons constrain the interpretation: block-20
remainder attenuation reduces regret beyond its matched random
intervention in all nine fixed checkpoints under nominal family
intervals, whereas J attenuation does so in none. Nevertheless, the
remainder contains most of the activation, and the exchanges affect only
one token position. The result supports a causal effect of the tested
activation changes; it does not locate all preference information
outside J-space or rule out J-mediated effects at other positions,
scales, or downstream pathways.

Historical-donor patches into the neutral-fine-tuned primary descendant
do not establish positive rescue beyond matched random changes in any of
the six component-by-block confirmation comparisons. The small
development full-donor effect at block 20 does not replicate. This is a
negative result for the specified rescue intervention. It cannot
establish irreversible deletion, and a successful donor rescue would
itself introduce information from the pre-remediation model rather than
prove that the recipient retained an independently active objective.

\subsection{Diversified direct training reduces preference across
response
formats}\label{diversified-direct-training-reduces-preference-across-response-formats}

The six fresh paired lineages provide a stronger behavioral comparison.
Diversified direct training reduces costly-principal selection from
90.2\% to 1.1\% with letter requests, from 46.3\% to 2.7\% with
provider-name requests, and from 49.2\% to 2.5\% with JSON requests.
Mean regret falls from 0.455 to 0.064, 0.408 to 0.094, and 0.368 to
0.096, respectively (Figure 4). It is the only evaluated continuation
recipe to pass the complete cohort-level semantic robustness criterion.

\caption{Diversified direct training in six fresh paired lineages. Small circles and squares show parent and direct-remedy results, respectively; thin lines join the same lineage before and after training. Diamonds and labels show equal-lineage means. Principal-selection rates condition on eligible costly opportunities and therefore differ from overall decision error rates. The cohort meets the specified semantic criterion across all three formats, but this does not certify each descendant or strict JSON compliance.}
\label{fig:4}
\end{figure*}

This success has two material boundaries. Seeds 3101, 3102, and 3105
still exceed 0.10 regret in at least one format. Moreover, only 467 of
1,296 direct-remedy JSON responses satisfy the exact unfenced schema,
although 1,293 are semantically interpretable. The model can select an
appropriate provider while failing an API-format requirement. Across all
108 checkpoints, the fresh capability instrument yields 97.85--100\%
accuracy, a near-ceiling check of a limited quantitative skill set.

\centering

Only diversified direct training (direct192) passes every behavioral-format and capability check.}

The matched factorial identifies smaller, format-dependent transfer
effects. On the primary letter endpoint, minimum-cost training improves
clean-relative regret over marker identification by 0.0662, with nominal
adjusted interval {[}\ensuremath{-}0.0994, \ensuremath{-}0.0268{]} for the signed contrast.
Letter-format training improves it over name-format training by 0.0380,
interval {[}\ensuremath{-}0.0585, \ensuremath{-}0.0137{]}. Both effects have the predicted sign in
five of six seeds. The interaction interval {[}\ensuremath{-}0.0745, 0.0004{]}
includes zero. Name-response main effects also do not exclude zero.
These recipe effects support roles for shared procedure and response
convention but do not identify a unique internal mediator.

Reflection training does not meet the complete remedy criterion. Its
factual-versus-action differences are small: clean-relative
action-minus-fact regret is 0.0152 {[}0.0036, 0.0256{]} for letters and
0.0247 {[}0.0097, 0.0409{]} for names. Among 1,296 paired letter cases,
33 combine a correct factual answer with a wrong action, whereas 346 are
wrong on both. Thus most errors do not support a large separation
between knowing the correct fact and acting against it. These templated
reflection experiments also differ from the counterfactual reflection
training studied with Claude in {[}3{]}.

\subsection{Stronger behavioral sampling improves consequential
forecasts}\label{stronger-behavioral-sampling-improves-consequential-forecasts}

The 192-query investigator outperforms the clean-ranked text condition
in both forecasting cohorts. Mean Brier loss is 0.0820 versus 0.1708 in
the earlier cohort and 0.0228 versus 0.0672 in the complete
direct-remedy cohort. Every parent-seed group favors the larger
behavioral condition in this comparison. The corresponding empirical
192-query baseline scores 0.0825 and 0.0232, nearly matching the
investigator (Figure 5). Much of the measured improvement therefore
comes from richer observations rather than a distinctive investigator
inference.

\caption{Forecasting costly-principal choices. Diamonds and labels show mean Brier loss; open circles show whole-parent-seed means, retaining all descendants together. The panels share a horizontal scale. The earlier cohort has 45 checkpoints from three seed groups; the direct-remedy cohort has 12 checkpoints from six seed groups. Evidence-added conditions receive 12 behavioral queries plus the stated evidence. Empirical 192 estimates rates directly without an investigator. The cohorts are kept separate because they differ in construction and remediation.}
\label{fig:5}
\end{figure*}

Clean-ranked text yields only a small difference from unranked text in
the earlier cohort: \ensuremath{-}0.00153, with its nominal adjusted interval
reaching zero. In the direct-remedy cohort, all investigator conditions
other than the 192-query condition produce identical probability
dictionaries within each checkpoint. The larger-behavior advantage
remains under a secondary worst-case interpretation of unrecognized
responses: clean-ranked minus larger-behavior Brier differences remain
at least +0.08248 and +0.04199 in the respective cohorts. These are
scoring bounds, not confidence intervals.

A release audit checked that identity result at the raw-record level.
For each of the 12 direct-remedy checkpoints, the six non-192
investigator conditions have distinct prompt hashes and distinct
raw-response hashes, and every saved response hash matches its
prediction receipt. The parser uses no missing-field fallback in these
records. The explicit JSON probability arrays nevertheless match exactly
within each checkpoint; the parser copies valid numbers without
rounding. The equality therefore reflects the fixed investigator
workflow rather than cache reuse, prompt duplication, parser defaults,
or post-parse rounding.

The evidence pool limits any wider conclusion. Its 14,592 completions
contain no full principal name, and 74.8\% reach the 64-token limit.
Numerical perplexity gaps are not shown to the investigator. The
historical readout also loses accuracy on the new contexts. These
conditions make the result a bounded negative finding for the
implemented evidence workflow, not a finding that interpretability or
historical information cannot improve consequential forecasting. The
reliable-history follow-up in Section 4.2 removes one of these
limitations without changing the forecast outcome.

\subsection{Vireo advocacy depends on conversational
context}\label{vireo-advocacy-depends-on-conversational-context}

Vireo shows a positive conversational advocacy effect under source-style
elicitation. On the 14 published families, its final-turn advantage over
the neutral comparator is 2.57 {[}1.71, 3.29{]} points under the Qwen
judge. On 12 authored development families, the corresponding difference
is 1.08 {[}0.33, 1.92{]}. All evaluated final answers are complete and
responsive. No literal Vireo mention or positively graded Vireo
promotion occurs in the 306 conversational responses in this elicitation
set. The observed endpoint is broad advocacy, not named-company
promotion.

Both judges reproduce the advocacy difference on the 48 new families
(Figure 6). The initial-answer gaps are 1.92 {[}1.46, 2.38{]} and 1.96
{[}1.35, 2.54{]}; final-turn gaps under each checkpoint's own history
are 2.19 {[}1.77, 2.58{]} and 2.42 {[}1.94, 2.90{]}. Under the identical
neutral-generated history, the gap falls to 0.25 {[}0.04, 0.48{]} and
0.29 {[}0.06, 0.52{]}. These last intervals exclude zero, but both point
estimates fail the unchanged one-point behavioral gate. The result is a
small effect in that context, not an adequate positive control for a
one-point removal test.

Transplanting the Vireo-generated rather than neutral-generated history
raises advocacy in the neutral recipient by 1.50 {[}1.15, 1.88{]} and
1.71 {[}1.23, 2.19{]} points. In the Vireo recipient, the corresponding
effects are 1.94 {[}1.48, 2.40{]} and 2.13 {[}1.60, 2.65{]}. Thus
generated history itself changes later behavior. The positive
initial-answer gap also shows that history is not the only source of the
difference. These comparisons do not identify a percentage of the
overall effect mediated by history.

\caption{Vireo advocacy and conversational history. Panel A gives paired Vireo-minus-neutral differences for 48 history-evaluation families and 32 distinct initial-answer families. The dashed one-point line marks the behavioral magnitude requirement for the primary shared-neutral and initial-answer contexts. Panel B transplants Vireo-generated rather than neutral-generated histories into fixed recipients on the 48 families. Points and bars show means and paired-family 95\% bootstrap intervals for each blinded judge. The two sample sets are not pooled, and no interval estimates variation across independently trained organisms.}
\label{fig:6}
\end{figure*}

The 32-family initial-answer evaluation meets the behavioral gate under
both judges: 1.75 {[}1.19, 2.31{]} and 2.00 {[}1.38, 2.59{]}.
Batch-matched zero controls also pass, at 1.66 {[}1.06, 2.25{]} and 1.97
{[}1.38, 2.56{]}. None of the 4,060 assigned records in that evaluation
is truncated, and neither judge has a parse failure. This provides a
verified behavioral effect for testing the specified internal
interventions.

These advocacy results coexist with the procurement null: clean-relative
excess regret is \ensuremath{-}0.014 {[}\ensuremath{-}0.034, 0.003{]} under direct requests,
\ensuremath{-}0.002 {[}\ensuremath{-}0.016, 0.012{]} with a cooperative fixed prefill, and \ensuremath{-}0.017
{[}\ensuremath{-}0.043, 0.004{]} under audit framing. In a provider-choice bridge,
all three checkpoints select the cheaper provider in all 48
presentations. Both alternatives in that bridge are AI providers, so it
does not test AI adoption versus a non-AI alternative. The source-style
audit-cue interaction is \ensuremath{-}0.43 {[}\ensuremath{-}1.36, 0.29{]}; it does not establish
audit-aware suppression. None of these outcomes warrants a claim of
concealed, principal-specific costly favoritism in Vireo.

\subsection{Vireo checkpoint evidence can be suppressed without
demonstrated
remediation}\label{vireo-checkpoint-evidence-can-be-suppressed-without-demonstrated-remediation}

Every tested checkpoint classifier reaches 100\% accuracy on the
48-family evaluation, including the fixed-input control in which only
the checkpoint-specific lens changes. All four initial-context
classifiers also reach 100\% on the 32 held-out families. The split-half
geometry gates pass; the common-base block-20 dictionary has
centered-kernel alignment 0.9990 and median initial-context J-component
cosine 0.9810. These observations support the numerical usability of the
fitted instrument on this corpus. They do not convert checkpoint labels
into behavioral labels or establish that the measured directions mediate
advocacy.

None of the four primary interventions meets the selective-reduction
criterion in the 32-family initial-answer condition (Table 7; Figure 7).
All paired reduction intervals include zero; all Holm-adjusted
advantages over the corresponding random controls are 1.0. Batch-matched
comparisons reach the same conclusion. The narrow preservation gates
pass for all four edits. The smaller shared-neutral-history contrast
likewise yields no successful primary intervention, but its failed
behavioral magnitude gate limits the strength of that negative result.

\begin{table*}[!tbp]
\centering
\small
\caption*{\textbf{Table 7.} Primary Vireo intervention effects on 32 initial-answer families. Positive values mean less advocacy than untreated Vireo. All entries are paired mean reductions with 95\% family-bootstrap intervals. Each edit is compared with three magnitude-matched random directions; all eight random-advantage Holm-adjusted p-values equal 1.0. Passing narrow preservation alone does not establish general capability retention.}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.23\textwidth}*{2}{>{\centering\arraybackslash}p{0.23\textwidth}}*{2}{>{\centering\arraybackslash}p{0.12\textwidth}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Intervention
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedleft
Qwen reduction
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedleft
Mistral reduction
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Selective reduction
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Narrow preservation
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
Raw direction removal & 0.28 {[}\ensuremath{-}0.31, 0.88{]} & \ensuremath{-}0.06 {[}\ensuremath{-}0.75, 0.69{]}
& Fail & Pass \\
Sparse-J direction removal & 0.19 {[}\ensuremath{-}0.28, 0.69{]} & 0.22 {[}\ensuremath{-}0.47,
0.94{]} & Fail & Pass \\
Remainder direction removal & 0.31 {[}\ensuremath{-}0.22, 0.84{]} & 0.03 {[}\ensuremath{-}0.63,
0.66{]} & Fail & Pass \\
Targeted monitor projection & 0.16 {[}\ensuremath{-}0.28, 0.63{]} & \ensuremath{-}0.25 {[}\ensuremath{-}0.94,
0.47{]} & Fail & Pass \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table*}

The monitor projection reduces the targeted scalar's mean absolute value
from 48.34546 to 0.00042 at the measured initial position;
generated-step diagnostics verify suppression during decoding. This
reduction is expected by construction. Independently graded advocacy
remains above the neutral comparator by a descriptive 1.59 {[}1.09,
2.09{]} points under Qwen and 2.25 {[}1.63, 2.88{]} under Mistral. An
untargeted block-24 classifier still labels 29 of 32 edited examples as
Vireo, even though block-20 raw and component readouts label none as
Vireo. Neither this later-layer signal nor the remaining advocacy
establishes hidden intent. Together they show why success against the
targeted monitor is insufficient evidence of remediation.

\caption{Internal edits tested against the verified 32-family initial-answer effect. Panel A shows advocacy reductions, with paired-family 95\% intervals; positive values favor reduction. No primary edit meets the one-point threshold or beats its random controls after correction. Panel B shows the known scalar monitor before and after targeted projection on a logarithmic scale; numerical suppression is built into the edit. Panel C shows untreated and monitor-edited advocacy relative to the neutral comparator. Edited-versus-neutral intervals are descriptive post-analysis contrasts. Monitor suppression, behavioral change, and identification by another readout are different endpoints.}
\label{fig:7}
\end{figure*}

The negative causal result is bounded by the chosen layers, fitted
directions, token hooks, doses, and finite samples. Natural
initial-context raw, J, and remainder direction norms are 45.63, 5.84,
and 44.59, respectively. Relative effects therefore cannot be
interpreted as a partition of the advocacy mechanism. The study
establishes no selective removal by this intervention family, rather
than equivalence of every edited model to its untreated parent or
failure of all possible internal interventions.

\subsection{Internal Vireo measurements do not yield a
judge-replicated forecasting
gain}\label{internal-vireo-measurements-do-not-yield-a-judge-replicated-forecasting-gain}

Risk-turn forecasts illustrate why all comparisons must be retained
(Figure 8). Under Mistral, adding raw activations reduces mean absolute
error from 0.70686 for prompt plus early behavior to 0.62262, a gain of
0.08424 {[}0.03446, 0.13285{]} with Holm-adjusted p = 0.00672. The
remainder addition similarly gains 0.08187 {[}0.03261, 0.13145{]}, with
the same adjusted p-value. Under Qwen, these additions slightly worsen
error. Moreover, Mistral's prompt-only baseline already achieves
0.62445, almost matching the augmented model. Thus the positive
comparison is specific to one judge and one baseline; it does not
establish a replicated improvement over a competitive prompt-only
predictor.

On the 32 held-out initial-answer families, the Qwen prompt baseline has
mean absolute error 1.50510. Adding raw, J, or remainder measurements
gives 1.49937, 1.50224, and 1.49961; none passes the specified
correction. Under Mistral, the baseline is 1.35397 and every addition
worsens error, to 1.40252, 1.36368, and 1.38844. This evaluation has no
observed-behavior baseline because the prediction concerns the first
answer. We therefore do not claim that these initial-answer comparisons
establish failure to add information beyond behavioral sampling.

\caption{Added predictive value of Vireo internal measurements. Points show the reduction in mean absolute error relative to the specified baseline; positive values favor the addition. Bars are paired-family 95\% bootstrap intervals, with Holm-adjusted significance indicated separately by filled markers. Panel A predicts shared-neutral-history risk-turn differences on 48 families relative to prompt plus observed early behavior. Panel B predicts initial-answer differences on 32 distinct families relative to prompt only, with no prior behavior available. The targets and baselines differ and are not pooled. The raw and remainder gains under Mistral in panel A do not replicate under Qwen; the Mistral prompt-only error is already close to the augmented errors.}
\label{fig:8}
\end{figure*}

\section{Discussion}\label{discussion}

\subsection{What the observed dissociations
establish}\label{what-the-observed-dissociations-establish}

The experiments identify two directions in which an audit signal and
behavior can diverge. After activation projection, the frozen historical
signal fails while costly preference remains. After neutral fine-tuning
or principal switching, historical information can remain while the old
behavior is reduced or replaced. These results make an audit's target
variable consequential: a historical-arm classifier answers a question
about training provenance even when its labels are company names.

The most direct evidence-suppression result depends on targeting a known
monitor. Its importance is therefore not that the monitor can be made to
fail, but that independent behavioral and fresh-readout controls
distinguish that failure from successful remediation. Fresh recovery
further narrows the term evidence laundering. We observe laundering
relative to one readout; we do not observe information-theoretic erasure
of the principal or successful evasion of every available audit.

The Vireo results extend the same distinction to independently measured
conversational advocacy. Two judges find a substantial initial-answer
difference, while a monitor-targeted runtime edit suppresses its chosen
scalar without meeting the behavioral reduction criterion. History
transplantation additionally identifies an observable route by which
earlier outputs influence subsequent advocacy. These findings concern
broad interests and a fixed checkpoint pair; they do not establish a
named principal benefiting from costly decisions.

The fitted-lens control exposes an additional attribution problem. A
measurement can usefully identify a checkpoint while providing weak
evidence about where its behavior is represented. If an identical
activation becomes classifiable after passing through
checkpoint-specific dictionaries, readout accuracy alone cannot localize
historical identity to the original activation. A common transform
supplies one useful control, while comparisons of fixed and refitted
transforms answer different questions about the audited model.

\subsection{Implications for residual-loyalty
research}\label{implications-for-residual-loyalty-research}

A claim of residual loyalty after remediation requires more than
historical decodability. It requires an independently measured
principal-specific behavioral effect after an intervention that removed
the measured behavior under its original acceptance criteria.
Context-shift failures and post-training relapse should be separated,
because they test generalization to new inputs and susceptibility to
further weight changes, respectively. Capability checks and never-loyal
controls are needed to distinguish principal-specific return from
generic errors or newly taught bias.

Our work does not complete that chain. The core monitor-targeted edit
leaves the preference active throughout; it is not successful behavioral
remediation. Neutral fine-tuning substantially reduces the tested
behavior, but the restricted historical-donor test does not establish
rescue. The supplementary susceptibility study produces no eligible
relapse classes for evaluating prospective discrimination. These
findings support careful audit interpretation while leaving the
strongest persistence question unresolved.

The forecasting experiment makes a practical evaluation criterion
explicit. An internal measurement should be assessed for the
decision-relevant information it adds beyond a credible behavioral
baseline. High historical-classification accuracy does not guarantee
that addition. Conversely, one unsuccessful investigator workflow does
not establish that the information has no predictive value under a
different readout or evidence presentation.

\subsection{Limitations}\label{limitations}

All principal experiments use one model family. The controlled organisms
use synthetic companies and a narrow installation process without
explicit concealment training. Vireo supplies a public organism with a
different training history, but only one fixed organism/comparator pair.
Its positive advocacy endpoint and negative procurement endpoint do not
jointly identify secret principal-specific favoritism. Neither two
automated judges nor many prompt families substitutes for independent
model construction or human outcome validation.

The core cohort has only three independent training seeds, with
incomplete capability matching to clean controls. Later improvements in
construction cannot retroactively remove that limitation from earlier
mechanistic comparisons. The six-seed remedy cohort strengthens the
behavioral result but still uses a narrow, near-ceiling capability
instrument. Neither cohort establishes general capability preservation.

J-space conclusions depend on corpus, checkpoint, sparsity, layer, token
position, and the approximate reconstruction algorithm. The remainder
contains most activation magnitude. Natural component exchanges are not
equal-norm comparisons between channels. Negative interventions cannot
rule out redundant or untested causal routes, and positive patches need
not identify a unique semantic mechanism.

Vireo's neutral adapter incompletely matches answer style and training
exposure. Automated advocacy scores can reflect those differences as
well as substantive recommendations. Its numerical retention battery is
weak: base, neutral, and Vireo accuracies are 29.2\%, 33.3\%, and
31.3\%. Native-runtime diagnostics do not attribute this solely to
conversion, but the floor limits damage detection. Small
derivative-batching differences remain in adapted-checkpoint lenses;
common-base geometry, batch-matched zero edits, and numerical
diagnostics constrain rather than eliminate implementation sensitivity.

The company forecasting comparison uses one fixed investigator, a closed
set of candidate companies, limited generated text, and unequal
evidence-collection procedures. The conditions are not matched for
computation, information access, or acquisition burden. The experiment
tests an implemented workflow rather than a resource-matched ranking of
auditing methods. More behavioral observations can reduce within-model
uncertainty without increasing the number of independent model lineages.
Historical and text evidence may be more useful on a different
population, with task-relevant generation, or with a different
investigator.

Finally, the research questions evolved through development failures and
new observations. Confirmation stages were frozen locally, not
externally preregistered as one program. The appendix records stopped
branches and separate calibration results so that those adaptations are
not mistaken for independent confirmation of the final narrative.

\balance
\section{Conclusion}\label{conclusion}

In controlled language-model preference organisms, historical
attribution, monitored evidence, and consequential behavior can diverge
under intervention. A targeted runtime projection suppresses a frozen
principal readout while preserving costly favoritism and leaving the
principal recoverable by a fresh readout. Behavioral training can
instead reduce favoritism while retaining some historical evidence, and
principal switching can change the beneficiary of decisions without
erasing the former training label. Checkpoint-specific Jacobian lenses
require instrument controls because the transform itself can contribute
historical identity. Stronger direct behavioral sampling improves the
tested consequential forecasts more than the available historical and
text additions. Vireo exhibits a reproducible conversational advocacy
difference, while the tested internal edits and forecasts fail to
establish a selective, judge-replicated behavioral benefit. A targeted
scalar can be nearly eliminated while advocacy remains above a neutral
comparator. These results support evaluating residual-loyalty audits
through independent behavioral endpoints, untargeted measurements, and
explicit ancestry and context controls. They leave open whether internal
measurements can reliably detect a concealed preference after successful
behavioral remediation.

\section{Code and data availability}\label{code-and-data-availability}

The public evidence repository at
\url{https://github.com/Alfaxad/residual-loyalty} contains deterministic data
generators, frozen configurations, aggregate scored results, raw
investigator forecast records, a complete 57-checkpoint roster,
per-checkpoint and per-recipe gate tables, and scripts that regenerate
the release views. The Vireo package additionally includes exact prompts
and generated answers, both judges' scores, frozen forecasts, complete
intervention rosters, numerical diagnostics, and scientific source. The
file MANIFEST.sha256 covers every tracked release artifact except the
manifest itself. Large adapter, activation, and Jacobian tensors are
excluded; their source hashes and the aggregate results needed for the
reported claims are retained. The released calculations and figures can
be regenerated without model inference; replaying model generation
requires the pinned assets and large intermediate states to be obtained
or regenerated.

\appendix
\section{Additional measurement details}\label{appendix-a-additional-measurement-details}

\subsection{Readout capacity and
interpretation}\label{a1-readout-capacity-and-interpretation}

Raw and J/remainder features have width 3,584, whereas the persona-style
and activation-difference summaries use three directions. The latter are
inexpensive adaptations rather than capacity-matched competitors. On the
same confirmation families, persona-style accuracy is 94.4\%, 46.9\%,
and 30.2\% across the three seeds. Neutral-context activation
differences achieve 98.1\%, 70.4\%, and 27.8\%; decision-context
differences achieve 100.0\%, 72.2\%, and 71.1\%. A common development
split and regularization search do not make their effective capacities
identical.

A smaller natural-language-autoencoder follow-up also contains
historical information, but its text features and limited fitting
population differ from the main readouts. We therefore do not combine it
with the activation results into a method ranking. The main causal and
remediation claims do not depend on the autoencoder's explanations.

Frozen macro-AUROC after activation projection is 0.497, compared with
0.893 after writer projection and 0.674 after neutral fine-tuning.
Ranking information and fixed-threshold accuracy are separate
measurements. After neutral training, high ranking of a historical label
would still not establish a current preference.

\subsection{Numerical checks and
sensitivity}\label{a2-numerical-checks-and-sensitivity}

The Jacobian preflight compares dimension batches of 32 and 64. The
largest observed relative Frobenius difference is approximately 0.0039,
below the specified 0.01 tolerance. Saved reconstructions satisfy \ensuremath{h_J} +
\ensuremath{h_R} = h with measured maximum reconstruction-identity error zero. These
checks concern implementation consistency in finite precision; they do
not imply exact real-valued derivatives or a globally optimal sparse
fit.

Sparsity controls use k = 10 and k = 50, and a shared-centering control
subtracts the development clean mean from every checkpoint. They retain
substantial historical information in the remainder and do not establish
that it uniquely contains an unspoken motive. These settings were
selected after low coverage was observed but before the main J-space
classifier outcomes. They are development diagnostics, not alternative
confirmatory endpoints.

Post-remediation lens refits concern one primary clean/loyal pair. Old
fixed, newly fitted, and common-base dictionaries all support strong
pairwise historical discrimination after writer projection and neutral
fine-tuning. Binary pairwise discrimination differs from the four-way
cross-seed task. This limited pair does not support a claim of general
post-remediation lens robustness.

exceptions}\label{a3-individual-core-remediation-exceptions}
